        <script id="save-data-connection-testing">
            function hasConnection() {
                return navigator.connection || navigator.mozConnection || navigator.webkitConnection || navigator.msConnection;
            }

            function createLink(src) {
                var preloadLink = document.createElement("link");
                preloadLink.rel = "preload";
                preloadLink.href = src;
                preloadLink.as = "font";
                preloadLink.type = "font/woff2";
                preloadLink.crossOrigin = "";
                document.head.insertBefore(preloadLink, document.head.firstChild);
            }

            var connectionDetail = {
                saveDataEnabled: false,
                slowConnection: false
            };

            var connection = hasConnection();
            if (connection) {
                connectionDetail.saveDataEnabled = connection.saveData;
                if (/\slow-2g|2g/.test(connection.effectiveType)) {
                    connectionDetail.slowConnection = true;
                }
            }

            if (!(connectionDetail.saveDataEnabled || connectionDetail.slowConnection)) {
                createLink("/static/fonts/HardingText-Regular-Web-cecd90984f.woff2");
            } else {
                document.documentElement.classList.add('save-data');
            }
        </script>

    (function () {
        if ( typeof window.CustomEvent === "function" ) return false;
        function CustomEvent ( event, params ) {
            params = params || { bubbles: false, cancelable: false, detail: null };
            var evt = document.createEvent( 'CustomEvent' );
            evt.initCustomEvent( event, params.bubbles, params.cancelable, params.detail );
            return evt;
        }

        CustomEvent.prototype = window.Event.prototype;

        window.CustomEvent = CustomEvent;
    })();
</script>

                                return !!document.querySelector(script.match) && !((moduleSupport && script.nomodule) || (!moduleSupport && script.module));
                            }).forEach(function (script) {
                                loader.insert(loader.createScript(script));
                            });
                        }
                    }, false);
                }
            };
            loader.init();
        }
    })(window, document);
</script>

    <meta name="dc.description" content="The ability to print and pattern all the components that make up a tissue (cells and matrix materials) in three dimensions to generate structures similar to tissues is an exciting prospect of bioprinting. However, the majority of the matrix materials used so far for bioprinting cannot represent the complexity of natural extracellular matrix (ECM) and thus are unable to reconstitute the intrinsic cellular morphologies and functions. Here, we develop a method for the bioprinting of cell-laden constructs with novel decellularized extracellular matrix (dECM) bioink capable of providing an optimized microenvironment conducive to the growth of three-dimensional structured tissue. We show the versatility and flexibility of the developed bioprinting process using tissue-specific dECM bioinks, including adipose, cartilage and heart tissues, capable of providing crucial cues for cells engraftment, survival and long-term function. We achieve high cell viability and functionality of the printed dECM structures using our bioprinting method. The application of 3D printing to biomaterials presents interesting possibilities for tissue engineering. Here, the authors show that a printing medium derived from an extracellular matrix can be applied to printing tissue analogues with enhanced cell compatibility."/>

        <div class="c-article-body">
            <section aria-labelledby="Abs1" data-title="Abstract" lang="en"><div class="c-article-section" id="Abs1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Abs1">Abstract</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Abs1-content"><p>The ability to print and pattern all the components that make up a tissue (cells and matrix materials) in three dimensions to generate structures similar to tissues is an exciting prospect of bioprinting. However, the majority of the matrix materials used so far for bioprinting cannot represent the complexity of natural extracellular matrix (ECM) and thus are unable to reconstitute the intrinsic cellular morphologies and functions. Here, we develop a method for the bioprinting of cell-laden constructs with novel decellularized extracellular matrix (dECM) bioink capable of providing an optimized microenvironment conducive to the growth of three-dimensional structured tissue. We show the versatility and flexibility of the developed bioprinting process using tissue-specific dECM bioinks, including adipose, cartilage and heart tissues, capable of providing crucial cues for cells engraftment, survival and long-term function. We achieve high cell viability and functionality of the printed dECM structures using our bioprinting method.</p></div></div></section>

                <div class="main-content" >
                    
                        <section data-title="Introduction"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec1">Introduction</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec1-content"><p>The ability to print tissue analogue structures through delivering living cells with appropriate material in a defined and organized manner, at the right location, in sufficient numbers, and within the right environment is critical for several emerging technologies. These technologies include, tissue-engineering scaffolds<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 1" title="Griffith, L. G. &amp; Naughton, G. Tissue engineering—current challenges and expanding opportunities. Science 295, 1009–1014 (2002)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR1" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e647">1</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 2" title="Gaetani, R. et al. Cardiac tissue engineering using tissue printing technology and human cardiac progenitor cells. Biomaterials 33, 1782–1790 (2012)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR2" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e650">2</a></sup>, cell-based sensors<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 3" title="Falconnet, D., Csucs, G., Michelle Grandin, H. &amp; Textor, M. Surface engineering approaches to micropattern surfaces for cell-based assays. Biomaterials 27, 3044–3063 (2006)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR3" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e654">3</a></sup>, drug/toxicity screening<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 4" title="Chang, R., Nam, J. &amp; Sun, W. Direct cell writing of 3D microorgan for in vitro pharmacokinetic model. Tissue Eng. Part C Methods 14, 157–166 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR4" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e658">4</a></sup> and tissue or tumour models<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 5" title="Fischbach, C. et al. Engineering tumors with 3D scaffolds. Nat. Methods 4, 855–860 (2007)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR5" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e662">5</a></sup>. The concept of tissue or organ printing, often described as bioprinting<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 6" title="Boland, T., Mironov, V., Gutowska, A., Roth, E. A. &amp; Markwald, R. R. Cell and organ printing 2: Fusion of cell aggregates in three-dimensional gels. Anat. Rec. A Discov. Mol. Cell. Evol. Biol. 272A, 497–502 (2003)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR6" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e666">6</a></sup>, is essentially an extension of the idea that uses additive manufacturing methods to build complex scaffold structures via a layer-by-layer process<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 7" title="Derby, B. Bioprinting: inkjet printing proteins and hybrid cell-containing materials and structures. J. Mater. Chem. 18, 5717–5721 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR7" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e671">7</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8" title="Kundu, J., Shim, J.-H., Jang, J., Kim, S.-W. &amp; Cho, D.-W. An additive manufacturing-based PCL–alginate–chondrocyte bioprinted scaffold for cartilage tissue engineering. J. Tissue Eng. Regen. Med. doi:10.1002/term.1682 (2013)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR8" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e674">8</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 9" title="Jung, J. W. et al. Projection image-generation algorithm for fabrication of a complex structure using projection-based microstereolithography. Int. J. Precis. Eng. Man. 13, 445–449 (2012)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR9" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e677">9</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 10" title="Seol, Y.-J., Kang, T.-Y. &amp; Cho, D.-W. Solid freeform fabrication technology applied to tissue engineering with various biomaterials. Soft Matter 8, 1730–1735 (2012)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR10" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e680">10</a></sup>. A crucial aspect of bioprinting is that the printing process must be cytocompatible, as it requires the dispensing of cell-containing media. This restriction reduces the choice of materials because of the necessity to operate in an aqueous or aqueous gel environment<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 11" title="Derby, B. Printing and prototyping of tissues and scaffolds. Science 338, 921–926 (2012)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR11" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e684">11</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 12" title="Ferris, C., Gilmore, K., Wallace, G. &amp; Panhuis, M. Biofabrication: an overview of the approaches used for printing of living cells. Appl. Microbiol. Biotechnol. 97, 4243–4258 (2013)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR12" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e687">12</a></sup>. In extrusion-based printing, hydrogels that are solidified through either thermal processes or post-print cross-linking are being used for printing of cells to produce diverse tissues ranging from liver to bone using materials such as gelatin<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 13" title="Wang, X. et al. Generation of three-dimensional hepatocyte/gelatin structures with rapid prototyping system. Tissue Eng. 12, 83–90 (2006)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR13" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e691">13</a></sup>, gelatin/chitosan<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 14" title="Chang, R., Nam, J. &amp; Sun, W. Effects of dispensing pressure and nozzle diameter on cell survival from solid freeform fabrication-based direct cell writing. Tissue Eng. A 14, 41–48 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR14" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e695">14</a></sup>, gelatin/alginate<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 15" title="Yan, Y. et al. Direct construction of a three-dimensional structure with cells and hydrogel. J. Bioact. Compat. Polym. 20, 259–269 (2005)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR15" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e699">15</a></sup>, gelatin/fibrinogen<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 16" title="Xu, W. et al. Rapid prototyping three-dimensional cell/gelatin/fibrinogen constructs for medical regeneration. J. Bioact. Compat. Pol. 22, 363–377 (2007)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR16" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e703">16</a></sup>, Lutrol F127/alginate<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17" title="Fedorovich, N. E., De Wijn, J. R., Verbout, A. J., Alblas, J. &amp; Dhert, W. J. A. Three-dimensional fiber deposition of cell-laden, viable, patterned constructs for bone tissue printing. Tissue Eng. Part A 14, 127–133 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR17" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e708">17</a></sup> and alginate<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18" title="Fedorovich, N. E. et al. Biofabrication of osteochondral tissue equivalents by printing topologically defined, cell-laden hydrogel scaffolds. Tissue Eng. Part C. Methods 18, 33–44 (2012)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR18" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e712">18</a></sup>. However, there are some concerns over the outcomes from these studies, such as the use of harsh cross-linking agents, like glutaraldehyde<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 14" title="Chang, R., Nam, J. &amp; Sun, W. Effects of dispensing pressure and nozzle diameter on cell survival from solid freeform fabrication-based direct cell writing. Tissue Eng. A 14, 41–48 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR14" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e716">14</a></sup>. Elsewhere, osteogenic differentiation was not prominent on alginate gel and no differentiation was observed on Lutrol F127 (ref. <a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17" title="Fedorovich, N. E., De Wijn, J. R., Verbout, A. J., Alblas, J. &amp; Dhert, W. J. A. Three-dimensional fiber deposition of cell-laden, viable, patterned constructs for bone tissue printing. Tissue Eng. Part A 14, 127–133 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR17" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e719">17</a>). In addition, when alginate gel was used for printing of a cell-printed structure, only a minor fraction of cells in the construct could differentiate towards osteogenic lineage<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17" title="Fedorovich, N. E., De Wijn, J. R., Verbout, A. J., Alblas, J. &amp; Dhert, W. J. A. Three-dimensional fiber deposition of cell-laden, viable, patterned constructs for bone tissue printing. Tissue Eng. Part A 14, 127–133 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR17" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e723">17</a></sup>. Normally, cells remain located specifically in their original deposited position during the whole culture period, as they are unable to adhere or degrade the surrounding alginate gel matrix<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 19" title="Fedorovich, N. E. et al. Hydrogels as extracellular matrices for skeletal tissue engineering: state-of-the-art and novel application in organ printing. Tissue Eng. 13, 1905–1925 (2007)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR19" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e727">19</a></sup>. This limited interaction between the cells within the gel can be explained by the noninteractive nature of alginate. Thus, although there were some successful reports about bioprinting of cell-printed structure, minimal cells–material interactions and inferior tissue formation are the foremost concerns.</p><p>Actually, these materials cannot represent the complexity of natural extracellular matrices (ECMs) and thus are inadequate to recreate a microenvironment with cell–cell connections and three-dimensional (3D) cellular organization that are typical of living tissues. Consequently, the cells in those hydrogels cannot exhibit intrinsic morphologies and functions of living tissues <i>in vivo</i>. It is thus ideal if cells are provided the natural microenvironment similar to their parent tissue. Decellularized extracellular matrix (dECM) is the best choice for doing so, as no natural or man-made material can recapitulate all the features of natural extracellular matrix (ECM)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20" title="Sellaro, T. L. et al. Maintenance of human hepatocyte function in vitro by liver-derived extracellular matrix gels. Tissue Eng. Part A 16, 1075–1082 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e737">20</a></sup>. Moreover, ECM of each tissue is unique in terms of composition and topology, which is generated through dynamic and reciprocal interactions between the resident cells and microenvironment<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Frantz, C., Stewart, K. M. &amp; Weaver, V. M. The extracellular matrix at a glance. J. Cell Sci. 15, 4195–4200 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e741">21</a></sup>. Recent studies of cells and ECM isolated from tissues and organs highlight the necessity of tissue specificity for preserving selected cell functions and phenotype<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20" title="Sellaro, T. L. et al. Maintenance of human hepatocyte function in vitro by liver-derived extracellular matrix gels. Tissue Eng. Part A 16, 1075–1082 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e745">20</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 22" title="Petersen, T. H. et al. Tissue-engineered lungs for in vivo implantation. Science 329, 538–541 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR22" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e748">22</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 23" title="Uygun, B. E. et al. Organ reengineering through development of a transplantable recellularized liver graft using decellularized liver matrix. Nat. Med. 16, 814–821 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR23" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e751">23</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Ott, H. C. et al. Regeneration and orthotopic transplantation of a bioartificial lung. Nat. Med. 16, 927–933 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e754">24</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 25" title="Flynn, L. E. The use of decellularized adipose tissue to provide an inductive microenvironment for the adipogenic differentiation of human adipose-derived stem cells. Biomaterials 31, 4715–4724 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR25" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e757">25</a></sup>. The dECM materials are harvested and typically processed as two-dimensional (2D) scaffolds from various tissues, including skin<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 26" title="Chen, R.-N., Ho, H.-O., Tsai, Y.-T. &amp; Sheu, M.-T. Process development of an acellular dermal matrix (ADM) for biomedical applications. Biomaterials 25, 2679–2686 (2004)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR26" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e761">26</a></sup>, small intestinal submucosa<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 27" title="Badylak, S. F. et al. The use of xenogeneic small intestinal submucosa as a biomaterial for Achille's tendon repair in a dog model. J. Biomed. Mater. Res. 29, 977–985 (1995)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR27" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e766">27</a></sup>, where at the initial stages the infiltrating or seeded cell populations depend on diffusion of oxygen and nutrient for their survival until a supporting vascular network develops. However, printing tissue analogue structures requires a fabrication approach to devise a highly open porous 3D structure to allow the flow of nutrients<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 11" title="Derby, B. Printing and prototyping of tissues and scaffolds. Science 338, 921–926 (2012)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR11" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e770">11</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 28" title="Wang, X., Yan, Y. &amp; Zhang, R. Rapid prototyping as a tool for manufacturing bioartificial livers. Trends Biotechnol. 25, 505–513 (2007)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR28" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e773">28</a></sup>. Organ transplantation in an orthotopic location is considered as a definitive treatment for end-stage organ failure<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 23" title="Uygun, B. E. et al. Organ reengineering through development of a transplantable recellularized liver graft using decellularized liver matrix. Nat. Med. 16, 814–821 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR23" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e777">23</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Ott, H. C. et al. Regeneration and orthotopic transplantation of a bioartificial lung. Nat. Med. 16, 927–933 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e780">24</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 29" title="Baptista, P. M. et al. The use of whole organ decellularization for the generation of a vascularized liver organoid. Hepatology. 53, 604–617 (2011)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR29" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e783">29</a></sup>. However, the demand for transplantable organs surpasses the number of available donor organs, compelling the manufacture of tissue or organs to address this unmet demand.</p><p>Here we develop a bioprinting method for printing of cell-laden structure with novel dECM bioink capable of providing an optimized microenvironment conducive to the growth of 3D structured tissue, where the intrinsic cellular morphologies and functions can be reconstituted. We show the versatility and the functionality of our bioprinting process using various types of dECMs bioinks, including adipose, cartilage and heart tissues. As applications of these functional cell-printed constructs, we observe higher-order assembly of these cellular constructs with organized spatial patterns and tissue-specific gene expression. A key advantage of this methodology is the application of tissue-specific ECM, providing crucial cues for cells engraftment, survival and long-term function.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Results"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec2-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec2">Results</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec2-content"><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec3">Preparation of dECM bioink</h3><p>The method for printing of cell-laden construct using dECM bioink consists of a few steps (<a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/ncomms4935#Fig1">Fig. 1</a>). Decellularization of the ECM material is the initial step and practically, the goal is to maximize the removal of cellular material, while minimizing ECM loss and damage<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30" title="Crapo, P. M., Gilbert, T. W. &amp; Badylak, S. F. An overview of tissue and whole organ decellularization processes. Biomaterials 32, 3233–3243 (2011)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR30" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e805">30</a></sup>. We successfully decellularized the adipose (adECM), cartilage (cdECM) and heart (hdECM) tissue after harvesting with a combination of physical, chemical and enzymatic process using slightly modified methods described elsewhere<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 31" title="Brown, B. N. et al. Comparison of three methods for the derivation of a biologic scaffold composed of adipose tissue extracellular matrix. Tissue Eng. Part C Methods. 17, 411–421 (2011)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR31" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e809">31</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 32" title="Ott, H. C. et al. Perfusion-decellularized matrix: using nature's platform to engineer a bioartificial heart. Nat. Med. 14, 213–221 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR32" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e812">32</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 33" title="Yang, Z. et al. Fabrication and repair of cartilage defects with a novel acellular cartilage matrix scaffold. Tissue Eng. Part C Methods 16, 865–876 (2010)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR33" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e815">33</a></sup>. Furthermore, 0.1% peracetic acid solution in combination with 4% ethanol was used in the last step of the decellularization method, which is frequently considered as a disinfection step<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 34" title="Freytes, D. O., Martin, J., Velankar, S. S., Lee, A. S. &amp; Badylak, S. F. Preparation and rheological characterization of a gel form of the porcine urinary bladder matrix. Biomaterials 29, 1630–1637 (2008)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR34" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e819">34</a></sup>. Efficiency of decellularization method was evaluated through DNA quantification assay and was found to be optimal for removing the cellular contents with ~98% reduction and only 39±15, 11±1 and 6.7±1.2 ng of DNA per mg of tissue remaining in the dECM of fat, cartilage and heart tissue, respectively (<a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/ncomms4935#Fig2">Fig. 2</a>). Hematoxylin and eosin (H&amp;E) staining confirmed the absence of cells and cell debris in the matrix after decellularization (<a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/ncomms4935#Fig2">Fig. 2b,c</a>) in all samples of dECMs, including those from the innermost regions of the processed tissues. Although the term decellularization has not been defined by quantitative metrics, &lt;50 ng dsDNA per mg ECM dry weight and lack of visible nuclear material in H&amp;E-stained tissue sections were reported to satisfy the objective of decellularization<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30" title="Crapo, P. M., Gilbert, T. W. &amp; Badylak, S. F. An overview of tissue and whole organ decellularization processes. Biomaterials 32, 3233–3243 (2011)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR30" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e830">30</a></sup>. Thus, it can be said that we could effectively decellularize all the tissue samples. Regrettably, xenogeneic ECM obtained from pig and other animals may contain the Galα1,3Galβ1,4GlcNAc-R (Gal) epitope, which is a major factor in the hyperacute rejection of pig organ transplants in primates<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 35" title="Daly, K. A., Stewart-Akers, A. A., Cordero, K., Johnson, S. A. &amp; Badylak, S. F. The effect of the {alpha}Gal epitope in the response to ECM in a nonhuman primate model. J Immunol. 182, 26 (2009)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR35" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e834">35</a></sup>. However, Gal epitope within porcine-derived ECM scaffold material was reported to elevate the serum antibody level, but no adverse reaction upon tissue remodelling was reported<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 35" title="Daly, K. A., Stewart-Akers, A. A., Cordero, K., Johnson, S. A. &amp; Badylak, S. F. The effect of the {alpha}Gal epitope in the response to ECM in a nonhuman primate model. J Immunol. 182, 26 (2009)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR35" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e838">35</a></sup>. Thus, it is expected that dECMs used in this study will not elicit any inflammatory reaction upon implantation in a recipient. We also estimated the ECM components, like collagen (COL) and glycosaminoglycans (GAGs), before and after decellularization (<a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/ncomms4935#Fig2">Fig. 2d–f</a>) with hydroxyproline assay and dimethylmethylene blue assay, respectively. The decellularization procedure adapted for heart tissue caused no noteworthy loss of COL and GAGs (<a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/ncomms4935#Fig2">Fig. 2e</a>). In case of adipose tissue, the COL content increased slightly and the GAGs content reduced moderately after decellularization (<a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/ncomms4935#Fig2">Fig. 2f</a>). As decellularization process removes all the cellular components along with lipids from the adipose tissue, COL contents seem to increase proportionately. For cartilage tissue, a significant loss of COL and GAGs were observed after decellularization (<a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/ncomms4935#Fig2">Fig. 2d</a>). The reason behind variation of the proportion of ECM components was the difference of decellularization processes adapted in this study<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30" title="Crapo, P. M., Gilbert, T. W. &amp; Badylak, S. F. An overview of tissue and whole organ decellularization processes. Biomaterials 32, 3233–3243 (2011)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR30" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e855">30</a></sup>. Cartilage has relatively denser ECM structure than that of heart, thus it requires more trypsinization and treatment with other enzymes, such as deoxyribonuclease (DNAse) and ribonuclease (RNAse), to remove all the cellular debris from ECMs. These enzyme treatments may be responsible for the reduction of COL and GAGs in decellularized cartilage tissue as trypsin treatment is known to cause partial removal of ECM constituents<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30" title="Crapo, P. M., Gilbert, T. W. &amp; Badylak, S. F. An overview of tissue and whole organ decellularization processes. Biomaterials 32, 3233–3243 (2011)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR30" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e859">30</a></sup>. Unfortunately, the natural organization of structural fibres is lost by decellularization and reduction to a soluble powder and advantages can be gained when other proteins (not identified) were present in the extracts as shown by work of others<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 36" title="Badylak, S. F. Xenogeneic extracellular matrix as a scaffold for tissue reconstruction. Transpl. Immunol. 12, 367–377 (2004)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR36" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e863">36</a></sup>. We have evaluated the presence of proteins or peptides through sodium dodecyl sulphate (SDS)–PAGE (<a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/ncomms4935#MOESM540">Supplementary Fig. 1</a>), which revealed the existence of various proteins within the dECMs used in this investigation and in accordance with other studies<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 37" title="Welham, N. V., Chang, Z., Smith, L. M. &amp; Frey, B. L. Proteomic analysis of a decellularized human vocal fold mucosa scaffold using 2D electrophoresis and high-resolution mass spectrometry. Biomaterials 34, 669–676 (2013)." href="/articles/ncomms4935#ref-CR37" id="ref-link-section-d103946528e871">37</a></sup>. Thus, the presence of various proteins and peptides will ensure to gain the advantages by preparing cell-printed constructs using dECMs bioink.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure decellularized after harvesting with a combination of physical, chemical and enzymatic processes, solubilized in acidic condition, and adjusted to physiological pH. The 3D cell-printed structure has applications in various border areas including tissue engineering, <i>in vitro</i> drug screening and tissue/cancer model.</p></div></div></figure></div><div
